mutex N=10 AG !(c1&c2) ~10^5 nodes, < 5 s 10–100× nodes, minutes
ring N=12 LTL fair-cycle < 30 s timeout

31.5 Dynamic reordering (sifting): cost vs benefit

  • Sifting moves one var up/down the order, recording node_count at each position, parking it at the minimum; repeat per var. Cost per pass = O(table size · n); can dominate if triggered too often.
  • Trigger policy: reorder only when live nodes exceed a growth-factor threshold since last reorder (e.g. 2×) AND not mid-ite (reordering reorders the shared DAG, so do it at fixpoint-iteration boundaries). After reorder: flush computed cache (handles invalid) — accounted as part of reorder cost.
  • Benefit accounting: a bench reports nodes-before / nodes-after / sift-time / steady-state-speedup. Win criterion: reorder pays off iff downstream image steps recoup the sift cost. On already-good static orders (interleaved counter), sifting yields ≤ 5% and should be off by default; on adversarial models it can cut nodes 10×. Default: reorder enabled, threshold conservative; CLI flag to force off for benchmarking the static order.

31.6 BMC SAT-call cost

  • Unroller builds I(s0) ∧ ⋀ T(si,si+1) ∧ ⋁ ¬p(si) as CnfFormula via Tseitin (linear in formula + k·|T|). CNF size grows linearly in k; the cost is the SatBackend::solve call, not the unroll.
  • Dominant cost = SAT solve, super-linear in k (clause count) and in unrolled var count. Bench the bundled DPLL/CDCL separately: decisions/s, conflicts, restarts; report time-to-SAT (counterexample found, easy) vs time-to-UNSAT (no CEX at depth k, hard — full search).
  • Incremental k: reuse learned clauses across k where the backend supports it; otherwise warm-start. Each k is a fresh solve; plot wall vs k — expect a sharp knee near the shallowest counterexample length.
  • Completeness threshold: safety G p is fully decided only once k reaches the reachability/recurrence diameter; record the empirical diameter per family so BMC-as-bug-finder (shallow k, fast) is distinguished from BMC-as-prover (k = threshold, expensive). Compare against BDD reachable on the same model: BMC wins for shallow bugs in wide state spaces; BDD wins for full proofs on well-ordered models.

31.7 Tail behavior on pathological orders

  • Worst case is real and unavoidable for some functions (e.g. the middle-bit multiplier output) — BDDs are exponential there regardless of order. Bench suite includes a known-hard fixture (bad-order counter, hidden-weighted-bit) to characterize, not "fix," the tail.
  • p50 vs p99 vs max across the model corpus: report all three. A regression that only moves p99 (a single adversarial model) is triaged differently from one that moves p50 (the common path).
  • Guardrails so the tail fails loud, not silent: hard node cap (abort with NodeLimitExceeded, never thrash), wall-clock timeout per check, and OOM-avoidance via the arena soft cap → GC → reorder → (still over) → bail. A pathological order must degrade to a clean error, never to a wrong verdict — and crucially never via a canonicity break (the GRAVEST failure class), which would corrupt equality and fixpoint convergence with no crash. Every tail test asserts the verdict matches NuSMV when it terminates, and asserts a clean limit-error when it doesn't.

Scalability Analysis

Cost model for the symbolic engine across the four scaling axes. The load-bearing fact: BDD size is order-sensitive, not input-size-bound — node_count ranges best-case O(n) to worst-case O(2^n) for the same function under different BddManager variable orders. Everything downstream (ite, and_exists, image, fixpoint count) scales in node_count, not in n directly.

32.1 Scaling vs. number of boolean variables n

n = sum of VarEncoding.levels.len() over all present+next vars; n_present = n/2. Bits per SMV var: Boolean→1, Enum(k)→ceil(log2 k), Range(a,b)→ceil(log2(b-a+1)).

Function class node_count vs n_present Notes
Symmetric (counter, parity, adder out) O(n)–O(n^2) order-insensitive
Comparators, equality x==y (interleaved) O(n) present/next interleave critical
Multiplier output bits Theta(2^(n/8)) provably exp every order (Bryant 1986)
Hidden-weighted-bit / "adversarial" 2^(n/2) worst, O(n) best order is the only lever
  • ite(f,g,h) is O(|f|*|g|*|h|) in node_count with the computed cache hot; products of large BDDs are the inner-loop cost, not n.
  • Each new BDD level doubles the potential node ceiling; whether it materializes depends on order. ⇒ adding one Enum(256) var = +8 levels = up to ×256 worst-case, ×1 if symmetric.
  • Pitfall: n is a poor predictor; teams sizing memory off n alone get blindsided. Size off measured node_count from bdd-stats.

32.2 Scaling vs. reachable-state count |R|

This is the symbolic engine's reason to exist (#279 is explicit; THIS engine never enumerates).

| |R| (reachable states) | Explicit (#279) | Symbolic (reachable() lfp) | |---|---|---| | 10^3–10^6 | feasible (hash set) | feasible, often slower (BDD overhead) | | 10^9–10^12 | infeasible (RAM) | routine if node_count(R) small | | 10^15–10^20 | impossible | feasible iff BDD stays compact (BCMD 1992) |

  • satcount(R, n_present) returns u128 precisely; the count can be 10^20 while node_count(R) < 10^5. The win is node_count decoupled from |R| — a regular state set compresses to a small DAG.
  • Failure mode: irregular reachable sets (no symmetry, arithmetic feedback) ⇒ node_count(R) blows toward |R|, erasing the advantage. Symbolic ≈ explicit then, but with worse constants.
  • Fixpoint iteration count for reachable = lfp Z. init OR Img(Z) is bounded by the reachability diameter (≤ |R|, usually << |R|), not |R|; each iterate cost ∝ node_count of the frontier.

32.3 Scaling vs. transition-relation size

Monolithic trans (Phase 5) BDD can dwarf init/R. Mitigation = conjunctive partitioning: trans = AND_i T_i, never built monolithically.

  • Image Img(S) = (exists present.(trans AND S))[next->present] via and_exists (Phase 6). Monolithic: one giant and_exists over the full trans ⇒ peak intermediate BDD = bottleneck.
  • Early quantification (Phase 12): quantify each present var as soon as the last T_i mentioning it is conjoined ⇒ shrinks intermediates, schedule chosen by min-support heuristic. Turns O(node_count(trans)*node_count(S)) peak into a sequence of small and_exists calls.
  • Scaling: peak image cost ∝ largest intermediate in the quantification schedule, not node_count(trans). A bad schedule re-grows what early-quant should have killed.
  • fairness: Vec<Bdd> and R(..) partitioning analogous (disjunctive for sums of behaviors).

32.4 Scaling vs. BMC bound k

smv-bmc path: Unroller emits I(s0) AND conj_{i<k} T(si,si+1) AND or_{i<=k} not p(si) as CnfFormula via Tseitin (Phase 11).

Quantity Growth in k Driver
CnfFormula.nvars O(k * n_present) one fresh var copy per step
CnfFormula.clauses `O(k * T
SatBackend::solve time worst-case O(2^nvars) NP-complete; CDCL median far better
  • BMC cost is in the SAT solver (#277 / bundled DPLL), exponential in the worst case but empirically near-linear in k for shallow bugs.
  • Memory is O(k * |T|_cnf) linear — BMC's memory advantage over BDDs. Falsification at depth d needs only k=d.
  • Completeness threshold (Phase 12): safety G p is fully decided once k ≥ reachability/recurrence diameter — but that bound can be ~|R|, so BMC is a bug-finder first, prover second.

32.5 BMC vs. BDDs — when each wins

Situation Winner Why
Deep, narrow bug (counterexample at large k) BMC SAT finds one path; BDDs must build whole R
No good variable order exists (multiplier-like) BMC sidesteps exponential node_count entirely
Property TRUE, need full proof BDDs fixpoint terminates with a certificate; BMC needs completeness threshold
Large but regular state space (10^15, compact R) BDDs satcount/reachable in seconds
Many short counterexamples / shallow bugs either both fast; BMC simpler to set up

Rule: BDDs prove, BMC refutes (cheaply, up to depth k). smv-cli exposes both (check vs bmc) deliberately.

32.6 The state-explosion ceiling

  • Hard ceiling = the point where node_count (BDD path) or nvars peak (BMC path) exceeds RAM. For BDDs this is reordering-deferrable but not removable; for adversarial functions it is provably exponential.
  • Diagnosis via node_count/bdd-stats time-series: monotone blowup during the reachability fixpoint = explosion onset; flat/decreasing = compact, scalable.
  • Symbolic pushes the ceiling from ~10^7 (explicit) to ~10^20 states only when structure exists; no structure ⇒ no compression ⇒ same wall, later.

32.7 Variable ordering — the dominant lever

  • A single reorder can swing node_count by orders of magnitude (best→worst is O(n)↔2^n). This dwarfs every other tuning.
  • Static: interleave each var's present/next bits (present/next VarMaps adjacent) so trans equalities stay O(n); group bits of one VarEncoding contiguously.
  • Dynamic: sifting (Phase 12) — move one level through all positions, keep the min-node_count spot; triggered when total nodes cross a growth threshold. Cost: O(levels * node_count) per sift pass, amortized against the savings.
  • Finding the optimal order is NP-hard ⇒ heuristics only; sifting is the accepted practical answer (CUDD lineage).

32.8 Memory — the hard wall

  • BDD node ≈ 16–24 B (level:u32, low/high: Bdd(u32), hash/next chain). 10^8 live nodes ≈ 2–4 GB.
  • Three consumers: node table (live R/trans/intermediates), unique table (hashcons buckets), computed cache. Cache is discardable under pressure (lossy) — first thing to shrink.
  • Mark-sweep GC (Phase 2) reclaims dead nodes; trigger on table-fill watermark. Without GC, intermediate image BDDs leak and OOM mid-fixpoint.
  • Cache too small ⇒ ite/and_exists lose memoization ⇒ blowup from O(|f||g||h|) toward exponential recomputation; cache too large ⇒ evicts live-node budget. Tune ratio (Phase 12), default cache ≈ 0.25× unique-table capacity.
  • Wall is non-graceful: BDD ops can spike peak nodes ×10 transiently (before reduction/GC), so headroom ≥ peak-intermediate, not steady-state, must be reserved.

32.9 Parallelism limits (single-threaded by design)

  • BddManager is single-threaded by design: &mut self on ite/and_exists/restrict ⇒ one writer; Bdd(u32) handles index one manager's table.
  • Rejected alternative — parallel/concurrent BDDs (lock-free unique table + shared computed cache, e.g. Sylvan-style): rejected because (a) the unique table hashcons is the canonicity guard — concurrent insert races risk a duplicate node ⇒ silently breaks ROBDD canonicity (the gravest failure class: wrong verdicts, no crash); (b) the computed cache wants lock-free sharing but fine-grained sync overhead often erases speedup at our target sizes; (c) Rust &mut aliasing rules make the simple single-owner design provably race-free, matching the index-arena choice. Determinism + canonicity > throughput here.
  • Sanctioned parallelism is outside the manager: BMC runs independent SatBackend::solve per k (embarrassingly parallel across bounds); multiple CTL subformula CtlChecker::check calls could fan out only with one manager per thread (no shared Bdd handles).
  • Consequence: BDD-path throughput is bounded by single-core ite/and_exists rate; scale via better orders and partitioning, not cores.

32.10 Bottlenecks → mitigations (summary)

Bottleneck Symptom Mitigation
Bad variable order node_count exp blowup static interleave + dynamic sifting (Phase 12)
Monolithic trans huge image intermediates conjunctive partitioning + early quantification
Cache thrash ite recompute, slow fixpoint tune cache/unique-table ratio; clear-on-reorder
Dead-node leak OOM mid-fixpoint mark-sweep GC on fill watermark
Deep bug, no good order BDD path stalls switch to bmc (SAT, linear memory in k)
BMC large k SatBackend::solve blows up incremental k, partitioned Tseitin, completeness threshold to stop
Irregular R (no structure) `node_count(R) ≈ R

Mental Models & First Principles

The following first-principles mental models underpin every design decision in this blueprint. When a performance or correctness question arises during implementation, trace it back to the applicable model below before reaching for an optimization.

MM1 — The Canonical DAG Principle (P1). Any two Boolean functions that are equal must share the exact same DAG node in the manager, and any two functions that are unequal must have different handles. This is not an optimization — it is the load-bearing invariant of the entire system. Every other operation (image computation, fixpoint iteration, CTL evaluation) depends on f == g iff handle(f) == handle(g). Violate this once and all downstream verdicts are silently wrong.

MM2 — Symbolic = Working with Predicates, Not States. The entire point of symbolic model checking (P3) is that we never enumerate the 10^20 reachable states. Instead, init, trans, and every intermediate set is a BDD representing a predicate over state bits. When you feel the urge to "loop over all states", that is a signal you have left the symbolic regime. The fix is always: encode the selection as a BDD constraint and use and_exists or restrict.

MM3 — ite as the Universal Primitive (P2). All Boolean operations, quantifiers, image steps, and fixpoint iterations reduce to recursive calls on ite(f,g,h). There is one place where correctness can fail (the ite recursion + cache logic), and there is one place to audit. This is not a limitation — it is the source of the engine's simplicity. When adding a new derived operation, always ask: "can I express this as one or more ite calls?" The answer is almost always yes.

MM4 — The Cache Is Lossy; Canonicity Is Not. The computed cache (memoized ite results) can be evicted at any time without changing correctness — a cache miss merely causes recomputation. The unique table (hashcons) can NEVER be incomplete or wrong: if a node (level, low, high) exists, it must be in the unique table, and if it is in the table, it must be the unique representative. The GC protocol follows from this: the cache is purged at GC time (cheap, safe), but the unique table is rebuilt from survivors only (expensive, mandatory).

MM5 — Fixpoints Are Monotone Sequences on a Finite Lattice. Every lfp/gfp used in this system (reachable, EX, EU, EG, Emerson-Lei fair states) is a monotone function on the finite lattice of BDDs over a fixed set of state-bit levels, ordered by implication. Kleene's theorem guarantees convergence in at most 2^n iterations (where n = number of state bits). In practice, convergence is typically 5–50 iterations on real models. If a fixpoint does not converge, it is a bug — assert on non-monotonicity rather than hanging.

MM6 — Variable Order Is the Dominant Complexity Driver. The same Boolean function can have a BDD of size O(n) or O(2^n) depending solely on the variable order (P1). No amount of algorithmic cleverness compensates for a catastrophically bad order. The practical heuristic (interleave present/next levels, keep related bits adjacent) works on most synchronous models. For anomalous models (multipliers, XOR-heavy), dynamic sifting (P7) is the escape valve. Always report node_count alongside verdicts so order quality is observable.

MM7 — Counterexamples Are First-Class (I-P8). A FAIL verdict without a concrete Trace is forensically worthless — you cannot validate the model or the spec. The onion-ring backward-reachability technique for CTL (Phase 8) and lasso extraction for LTL (Phase 10) are not optional polish; they are required output of any FAIL result. The Trace is also the primary artifact for the NuSMV differential oracle (Phase 13): a mismatch in verdict OR in trace validity is a bug.

MM8 — The SAT Backend Is a Seam, Not an Optimizer (ADR-16). BMC (Phase 11) depends on a SatBackend trait, not a concrete solver. The bundled DPLL is a "does it work" baseline — #277 (the companion project) is the real solver. The Tseitin encoder and unroller must produce correct DIMACS regardless of which SAT backend is plugged in. Never let BMC performance concerns leak into the CNF encoding; CNF correctness (equisatisfiability) is the non-negotiable invariant.

MM9 — The NuSMV Oracle Is the Truth Gate (I-P11). This project's "done" condition is not unit tests passing — it is NuSMV-agreement on a corpus of real models. The differential oracle (Phase 13) is the only external reality check. Any verdict mismatch is a bug in THIS implementation (NuSMV is the ground truth by project definition), until proven otherwise by examining the NuSMV source. Design every checker with oracle-comparability in mind: same semantics, same acceptance conditions, comparable trace lengths.

MM10 — no_std Is an Architectural Constraint, Not a Feature Flag. The core crates (smv-bdd through smv-logic) must build on embedded targets (thumbv7em-none-eabi) from day zero. Discovering a std dependency in Phase 10 means a rewrite of Phase 1 data structures. The discipline is: anything that uses alloc:: is permitted; anything that uses std:: must be behind a feature gate or moved to smv-check/smv-bmc/smv-cli. CI enforces this with a no_std build job (ADR-19, ADR-24).

Comparative Architecture Table

How this design compares to four real symbolic model-checking systems on the dimensions that matter most.

Dimension This Project (smv-bdd + smv-check) NuSMV 2.6 (FBK, C) CUDD 3.0 (Somenzi, C) — BDD library only Spot 2.11 (LRDE, C++) — LTL+automata focused ABC (Berkeley, C) — hardware-centric

BMC Tseitin CNF unrolling; SatBackend trait; bundled DPLL + #277 hook (P5/P6) Full BMC with loop encoding, LTL BMC, IC3/PDR; multiple SAT backends (MiniSat/CaDiCaL FFI) Not applicable ltlsynt / ltlcross use SAT internally; no standalone BMC ABC's primary verification mode: PDR/IC3, k-induction, interpolation

Input language NuSMV SMV subset: boolean / enum / range VAR, ASSIGN, TRANS, INVAR, CTL/LTL/INVARSPEC Full NuSMV 2.6 language: arrays, processes, COMPUTE, modular instantiation None (C API only) LTL formula strings + Kripke structure/HOA/LBTT automaton files BLIF/AIGER/Verilog/BTOR hardware netlists; no temporal spec language
Learning fidelity Builds every layer from scratch — highest learning return Reference ground truth; studying source code is an input to this project BDD engineering reference (cuddTable.c, cuddBddIte.c); source study prescribed Automata theory reference (GPVW node expansion); source study for Ph9 Hardware formal methods reference; not a primary input to this project
no_std portability Core crates: no_std + alloc by design; CI-enforced (ADR-19) C; requires OS (malloc, file I/O); no embedded profile C; requires system malloc; not designed for embedded C++; requires STL + OS C; requires OS
Differential oracle role N/A (this IS the implementation under study) Ground truth oracle for Phase 13 — verdicts must match on the corpus Not an oracle; studied for BDD implementation patterns SPOT's ltlcross can cross-check LTL verdicts; secondary check option Not an oracle for this project

NuSMV 2.6. The one decision it makes differently: a full modular input language (processes, arrays, COMPUTE, asynchronous interleaving) layered over the same symbolic engine family this project implements from scratch. Where it wins: any real verification job that needs the full SMV language surface today — multi-process async models, reused NuSMV model libraries, or a team that cannot afford to hand-roll a BDD package. This project takes the opposite bet deliberately: less language, more implementation depth, so NuSMV remains the differential oracle (Phase 13, §25) rather than a competitor.

CUDD 3.0. The one decision it makes differently: complement edges + a refcount-based manual Cudd_Ref/Cudd_RecursiveDeref API instead of an index arena with mark-sweep GC. Where it wins: any long-running process embedding millions of BDD operations per second where refcounting avoids GC pause entirely (M8's 100 ms mark-sweep pause has no analogue in a well-refcounted CUDD program) — real-time-adjacent EDA tool integration is CUDD's actual deployment niche. This project rejects manual refcounting because C-style Cudd_Ref bugs (missing/duplicate deref) are exactly the use-after-free/double-free class Rust's ownership model exists to eliminate (ADR-03).

Spot 2.11. The one decision it makes differently: automata (TGBA/parity) are the primary internal representation, with BDDs (via BuDDy) as a secondary encoding — the inverse of this project's BDD-primary, automaton-secondary design. Where it wins: LTL synthesis and translation research where the automaton is the deliverable (Spot's ltl2tgba/ltlcross toolchain), and where CTL is not required at all. This project needs CTL and LTL under one symbolic model (P5), so a BDD-primary architecture is the only one that serves both without two incompatible state representations.

ABC. The one decision it makes differently: hardware netlists (AIGER/BLIF/Verilog) as the native input with PDR/IC3 and k-induction as the primary safety-proof engines, BDDs demoted to an optional backend. Where it wins: industrial hardware verification at 10^60+ state scale where liveness (CTL/LTL) is out of scope and only safety (AG p) matters — IC3/PDR's frame-based reasoning outperforms BDD fixpoints by orders of magnitude there (Alt-B). This project needs liveness (EG/EU, LTL fair cycles) that IC3/PDR alone cannot decide, so BDD fixpoints stay primary.

Key takeaway: This project occupies the "build to understand" niche that none of the above tools fill — the goal is implementing the algorithms from their source papers, not reusing a library. NuSMV serves as external ground truth (not competition). The design departs from CUDD primarily in memory model (index arena + mark-sweep vs. refcount + tagged pointers) and from NuSMV primarily in simplicity (no processes, no arrays, no COMPUTE) and portability (no_std).

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

Derived estimates for the practical operating envelope of this engine. These numbers justify the design targets in the Performance Engineering section (Performance Targets table) and the Scalability Analysis section below.

Symbols: n = number of boolean state-bit levels; N_bdd = number of live BDD nodes; R = reachable-state count; k = BMC unrolling depth.

# Quantity Formula / Derivation Concrete estimate
C1 Node table RAM at 16M live nodes 16M × (4+4+4) bytes = 192 MB (level:u32, low:u32, high:u32) 192 MB; fits in 256 MB JVM-class heap
C2 Unique-table hash map overhead at 16M entries FxHashMap with 75% load factor: ≈16M / 0.75 × 16 bytes (k/v) = 341 MB ~340 MB; total package ≈530 MB for 16M nodes
C3 Cache (computed table) at default 8M entries 8M × (4+4+4+4) bytes (op,f,g,h each u32) = 128 MB 128 MB; cache:unique ratio ≈1:2 (typical Somenzi guidance)
C4 Full package RAM at target (16M nodes) C1 + C2 + C3 ≈ 530 MB + 128 MB = ~660 MB ≈700 MB with GC/auxiliary overheads; fits in 1 GB
C5 Maximum state count representable symbolically satcount uses u128; 2^127 ≈ 1.7 × 10^38 states Far exceeds 10^20 target; satcount is the bottleneck only beyond 2^64
C6 ite/apply throughput estimate Each ite call: 1 cache probe + (optionally) 1 unique-table insert + 2 recursive calls; empirical: ~2M ite calls/sec on 2020 laptop at 8M-node table (cache-warm) With 1M-node reachable set and 50 fixpoint iters: 50 × 1M ite calls = 50M ite ops ≈ 25 sec (within 60 sec target per §25)
C7 Number of state bits for n-process mutual exclusion n processes × ceil(log2(states_per_process)) bits; Peterson(n=2): 5 bits; n=10: ~20 bits 20-bit model: 2^20 = 1M potential states; BDD likely stays <100K nodes with good order
C8 Tseitin CNF size for BMC unrolling at depth k Clauses ≈ k × (clauses_per_transition); for 50-bit model with 200 gates/step: k × 200 × 3 clauses ≈ 600k clauses at k=1000 1M clauses at k=1000; MiniSat/CaDiCaL handles 1M clauses routinely in <1 sec
C9 Sifting time at 16M nodes (worst case) Sifting n variables × 2n swaps per var × O(nodes) per swap; n=50, swap_cost=O(0.01×16M)=160K ops: 50 × 100 × 160K = 800M ops ≈ 2 min (estimated) Single sift pass ≈ 2 min worst-case; dynamic reorder trigger at 1.5× growth avoids the worst (ADR-08)
C10 GPVW Büchi state-bit overhead Büchi automaton for LTL formula φ has at most 2^(2 φ
C11 GC reclaim threshold GC is triggered when live_nodes > gc_threshold (default 2^20); expected reclaim ≥25%; if reclaim <25%, threshold grows 1.3× per GC At 2^20 = 1M trigger: GC scans 1M entries, reclaims ~250K; table shrinks to ~750K nodes
C12 Differential corpus runtime estimate 50 test cases × avg 30 sec per case (BDD fixpoint + NuSMV invocation + comparison) = 25 min Full corpus run in CI: 30 min; fits in 1-hour CI budget

Design validation: C4 confirms the "fits in 1 GB" target is achievable at the 16M-node operating point. C6 shows the throughput is sufficient for the 60-second wall-time target on production-size models. C9 flags sifting as the potential bottleneck — the growth-threshold trigger (ADR-08) is the mitigation.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

These are the questions a senior formal-verification engineer would ask to probe real understanding of this system. If you cannot answer them in detail, you have a gap to close before this blueprint is internalized.

On BDD fundamentals:

  1. Why does fixing a total variable order make the BDD canonical? What breaks if you allow two nodes at the same level to use different orderings of their descendants? (resolves in §6 P1 + §13 Phase 1 canonicity test.)
  2. The two reduction rules (R1: eliminate nodes with low==high; R2: merge identical triples) are stated as simplifications. Prove that both are necessary — construct a BDD that is canonical under R1 but not R2, and vice versa. (resolves in §8 Domain Model reduction rules + §13 Phase 1 implementation.)
  3. Why is ite(f,g,h) sufficient as the sole primitive? Give a step-by-step derivation showing that and_exists(f,g,cube) can be expressed purely as nested ite calls (without using a separate and + exists). (resolves in §6 P3 + §14 Phase 2 BDD operations.)
  4. How does the computed cache interact with GC? Why must the cache be entirely purged on every GC sweep rather than just removing entries referencing dead nodes? What would go wrong if you tried the partial approach? (resolves in §7 INV-4 + §14 Phase 2 GC.)
  5. Explain the satcount formula for a non-terminal node n: count(n) = 2^(skip_low) * count(low(n)) + 2^(skip_high) * count(high(n)) where skip_x = level(child_x) - level(n) - 1. What does "skip" represent physically? What is the base case for terminals? (resolves in §11 Core Schema satcount signature + §14 Phase 2 satcount.)

On symbolic model checking:

  1. Write out the full lfp characterization of sat(E[f U g]) in terms of preimage and BDD operations. Why is this a least fixpoint (not greatest)? What initial set does the iteration start from, and what is the termination condition? (resolves in §18 Phase 6 fixpoint framework + §19 Phase 7 CTL.)
  2. What is the difference between the forward-image formula Img(S) = ∃V . T(V,V') ∧ S(V) and the pre-image formula PreImg(S) = ∃V' . T(V,V') ∧ S(V')? Which is used for reachability, and which for CTL EX? Why? (resolves in §18 Phase 6 reachability + §19 Phase 7 CTL EX.)
  3. The and_exists operation is described as "fused" conjunction-quantification. What is the concrete BDD-size advantage of fusion over the two-step exists(and(f,g), cube)? Give an example where the intermediate and(f,g) would be exponentially larger than and_exists(f,g,cube). (resolves in §14 Phase 2 and_exists + §18 Phase 6 image usage.)
  4. CTL EG p requires a greatest fixpoint. What would happen if you implemented it as a least fixpoint starting from p (same as EU)? Construct a 3-state Kripke structure where the lfp and gfp give different results for EG p, and show which is correct per the semantics. (resolves in §18 Phase 6 fixpoint framework + §19 Phase 7 CTL EG.)
  5. For LTL G(p → F q), negate and apply GPVW. What does the resulting generalized Büchi automaton look like? How many acceptance sets does it have? How does Emerson-Lei find the fair cycles in the product? (resolves in §21 Phase 9 LTL front end + §22 Phase 10 Emerson-Lei.)

On implementation and debugging:

  1. You run your engine on the Peterson mutual exclusion model and get "AG(¬(critical_1 ∧ critical_2)) HOLDS", but NuSMV reports "VIOLATED" with a counterexample. Describe your triage process. What is the first thing you check, and why? (resolves in §25 Phase 13 differential testing + Failure Modes and Recovery F5/F11.)
  2. Your BDD node count explodes by 10× after adding variable reordering. What are the three most likely causes, and how do you diagnose each? (resolves in §9 ADR-08 variable-ordering policy + §24 Phase 12 sifting.)
  3. The Tseitin transform produces equisatisfiable CNF. What exactly does "equisatisfiable" mean here? Is the resulting CNF equivalent to the original formula? Why does the distinction matter for BMC (specifically for the "no bug up to depth k" result)? (resolves in §8 Domain Model Tseitin transform + §23 Phase 11 BMC.)
  4. You observe that satcount returns a value that is 2× too large on one specific fixture. What is the most likely cause, and what does the fix look like in the code? (resolves in §11 Core Schema satcount signature + §14 Phase 2 satcount.)
  5. A fuzz campaign on BddManager::ite finds an input that causes node_count to return a different value from two structurally equivalent builds of the same formula. Which invariant has been violated, how would you detect it in CI without fuzzing, and what is the root cause in the implementation? (resolves in §7 INV-1 canonicity + §25 Phase 13 fuzzing.)

On architecture and design:

  1. ADR-03 chose u32 index handles over Rc<Node>. A colleague argues that Rc<Node> would make the code simpler by removing the need for a GC. Rebut this argument with at least three concrete failure modes of the Rc approach for a BDD manager. (resolves in §9 ADR-03 + §11 Core Schema Bdd/BddManager.)
  2. ADR-12 chose and_exists (fused relational product) over separate and + exists. For a model with 50 state bits and a conjunctively partitioned transition relation with 10 conjuncts, estimate the intermediate BDD size difference between the two approaches. (resolves in §9 ADR-12 + §18 Phase 6 image computation.)
  3. The SatBackend trait (ADR-16) is a seam between the Tseitin encoder and the SAT solver. What would you need to change in smv-bmc if you wanted to support incremental SAT solving (adding clauses one at a time across BMC depths)? Would the current trait signature support this, or would you need to extend it? (resolves in §9 ADR-16 + §23 Phase 11 BMC.)
  4. The project uses conjunctive partitioning of the transition relation (ADR-11). When would you switch to disjunctive partitioning? Give a concrete model class where disjunctive is strictly better, and explain why. (resolves in §9 ADR-11 + §24 Phase 12 partitioning.)
  5. Why does the LTL checker negate φ (building Büchi(¬φ)) rather than checking φ directly? What logical relationship makes this valid, and what would go wrong if you forgot the negation? (resolves in §21 Phase 9 LTL front end + §22 Phase 10 LTL checking.)

On operational and production concerns:

  1. A user reports that smv check hangs indefinitely on their 200-variable model. What resource-limit configuration knobs exist in the engine, and what is the correct user-facing advice? What verdict/error should the engine return when it hits the node limit? (resolves in §4 System Constraints caps + Threat Model T1.)
  2. The Phase 13 differential harness reports a verdict match but the counterexample traces differ in length. Is this a bug? What are the conditions under which a shorter trace from our engine is valid, and when would it be a bug? (resolves in §25 Phase 13 + Failure Modes and Recovery F10.)
  3. You are adding a new LTL operator (e.g., W (weak until), not currently in the grammar). List every file and section in the blueprint that must change, in dependency order. (resolves in §8 Domain Model grammar + §21 Phase 9 LTL front end.)
  4. A downstream user wants to embed smv-bdd as a no_std library in a real-time OS kernel with a fixed memory pool (no heap allocator). What would need to change in the BddManager implementation to support a fixed-size arena instead of Vec<Node>? (resolves in §9 ADR-19 + §11 Core Schema BddManager.)
  5. The Emerson-Lei algorithm computes fair states as a nested greatest fixpoint. On a model with 30 state bits and 5 fairness sets, estimate the worst-case number of BDD operations. Is there a case where a simpler algorithm (single-set Buchi) would give the same result? When exactly? (resolves in §18 Phase 6 fixpoint framework + §22 Phase 10 Emerson-Lei.)

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Each alternative below is a serious design that works in production for some problem class. The design space is not "this vs. bad ideas" — it is "this vs. other good ideas that are wrong for this project's goals."

Alt-A: Explicit-State Model Checking (#279 Architecture)

Description. Instead of BDDs, maintain the reachable state set as a hash set of concrete state vectors. CTL and LTL checking operate on the explicit state graph.

         ┌─────────────┐
         │ State Set   │  = HashSet<Vec<u8>>  (concrete states)
         │ (explicit)  │
         └──────┬──────┘
                │  BFS / DFS over T(s, s') function
                v
         ┌─────────────┐
         │  CTL/LTL    │  SPIN/NDFS/Tarjan on explicit graph
         │  Checker    │
         └─────────────┘

Strengths: No variable-ordering problem; counterexamples are trivially concrete states; no BDD package to implement; SPIN/PRISM/JPF regime. Fatal weakness for this project: Cannot handle 10^15+ state spaces; each state is a point, not a predicate. This is explicitly project #279 — a different blueprint. When this wins: Models with ≤10^6 states AND fine-grained interleaving (Petri nets, concurrent protocols with process interleavings) AND counterexample diversity is more important than state-space scale.

Alt-B: SAT-Only Verification (IC3/PDR Architecture)

Description. Replace BDD-based reachability and CTL with SAT-based property-directed reachability (IC3/PDR). No BDD package at all — the symbolic state representation is implicit in the SAT solver's clause database.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IC3/PDR Engine                   │
         │  Frames: F_0 ⊆ F_1 ⊆ ... ⊆ F_k  │
         │  Each frame: set of clauses       │
         │  (clauses ARE the state predicate) │
         └──────────────────────────────────┘
                │  SAT queries: F_i ∧ T ∧ ¬F_{i+1}
                v
         ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SAT Solver (MiniSat / CaDiCaL)   │
         └──────────────────────────────────┘

Strengths: State-of-the-art for safety properties (AG p) on industrial hardware; handles 10^60+ states; no variable ordering; ABC's primary mode. Fatal weakness for this project: CTL liveness properties (EU, EG, AF) and LTL model checking require BDD fixpoints or automata-based methods — IC3/PDR handles safety only. Learning IC3 is a different project (closer to #277, the SAT solver project). When this wins: Pure safety verification (AG p) on hardware netlists with millions of state bits; no liveness specs; counterexample-guided synthesis workflows.

Alt-C: Multi-Terminal BDDs (MTBDDs / ADDs) for Quantitative Specs

Description. Replace BDDs (Boolean-terminal: 0 or 1) with Algebraic Decision Diagrams (ADDs/MTBDDs) where leaf values are arbitrary elements of a semiring (real numbers, probabilities, costs). The BddManager becomes an AddManager parameterized over a semiring.

         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ AddManager<R: Semiring>                          │
         │  Nodes: (level, low, high)                       │
         │  Terminals: elements of R (not just {0,1})       │
         │  Operations: apply(op, f, g) where op: R×R→R     │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Strengths: Can represent probability distributions (PRISM's probabilistic model checking), cost functions, and weighted model counting without a separate tool. CUDD already supports this via MTBDDs. Fatal weakness for this project: Boolean model checking is the core competency — introducing a semiring parameter blooms the type system, changes the GC story (semiring elements may need lifetime management), and doubles the correctness surface. PRISM and PARAM handle probabilistic specs; this project tracks NuSMV (boolean). When this wins: Probabilistic model checking (Markov Decision Processes), quantitative analysis (average reward, probability of reaching a state), weighted verification.

Alt-D: ZDD-Based Encoding (Zero-Suppressed Decision Diagrams)

Description. Replace ROBDDs with ZDDs (Minato, 1993). ZDDs suppress the zero terminal instead of the identity cofactor, making them optimal for sparse Boolean functions and set-of-sets representations.

ROBDD reduction:  eliminate nodes where low(v) == high(v)   (identity cofactor)
ZDD reduction:    eliminate nodes where high(v) == 0         (zero terminal)

ROBDD optimal for: dense functions, state predicates
ZDD optimal for:   sparse sets (primes, combinations, covers)

Strengths: ZDDs can be 10–1000× more compact than ROBDDs for sparse functions (prime implicant covers, combinatorial sets with few "1" paths). CUDD supports ZDDs natively. Fatal weakness for this project: Symbolic state sets in model checking are NOT sparse — the reachable set of a hardware model is typically dense over the set of reachable bit patterns. ZDDs are well-suited for combinational optimization (SIS, espresso) and combinatorial enumeration, not fixpoint model checking. BDD and ZDD cannot be mixed in the same computation without conversion overhead. When this wins: Logic synthesis (prime implicant enumeration, two-level minimization), combinatorial counting, implicit representation of large combinatorial structures (graph coloring, tiling).

Deferred Features Register

Every PhaseDeferred marker used in 1.7 Deferred / stretch features anchors to a real phase heading below; each anchor resolves, the building phase is where the feature would land, and the un-defer trigger is the concrete signal that promotes it into the v1 path.

Feature Anchor Building Phase Un-defer Trigger
Zero-suppressed BDDs (ZDDs) and ADD/MTBDD numeric terminals §24 Phase 12 — Optimizations, partitioning, and fairness A downstream consumer needs sparse-set (prime-implicant-style) encodings, or probabilistic/weighted model checking (Alt-C) beyond the boolean ROBDD core.
IC3/PDR and k-induction proof engines §23 Phase 11 — Bounded Model Checking (SAT unrolling) Phase 13 benchmarks show the BMC completeness threshold is unreachable on a target model class and unbounded safety proofs are required (Alt-B territory).
Partitioned/disjunctive transition relations beyond the conjunctive default + image-computation MBM clustering §24 Phase 12 — Optimizations, partitioning, and fairness Phase 13 differential corpus shows conjunctive partitioning hitting the node cap on models with disjunctive next-state structure (e.g. multi-process interleaving).
Full LTL[*] / PSL / SVA assertion syntax §21 Phase 9 — LTL front end and LTL-to-Buchi translation A conformance fixture requires a PSL/SVA operator (e.g. a clocked assertion) not expressible in core LTL.
Streett/Rabin (beyond Büchi/Streett fairness) and parity acceptance §24 Phase 12 — Optimizations, partitioning, and fairness A target model's fairness constraints cannot be encoded as generalized Büchi and require a genuine Rabin pair.
Interpolation-based unbounded BMC and abstraction-refinement (CEGAR) §23 Phase 11 — Bounded Model Checking (SAT unrolling) Same trigger as IC3/PDR above, with interpolation-based invariant generation prioritized over IC3/PDR for the unbounded-proof path.
Parallel/multi-threaded BDD ops and concurrent GC §14 Phase 2 — BDD operations for model checking (quantification, relational product, GC) Measured single-threaded ite/apply throughput (Back-of-Envelope C6) becomes the bottleneck on target hardware and multi-core scaling is prioritized over the single-writer simplicity of C21 (System Constraints).

Glossary

Terms defined as used in this spec. Format: Term — definition; where it lives / why it matters here. Alphabetical within groups; cross-references in bold.

BDD package & data structures

  • ROBDD (Reduced Ordered Binary Decision Diagram) — rooted DAG over a fixed total variable order representing a Boolean function; "reduced" = both reduction rules applied. The data structure smv-bdd builds; the entire engine represents state sets and the transition relation as ROBDDs, never as enumerated states (vs #279). Synonym "BDD" used interchangeably; no other diagram variant (ZDD/ADD) in scope.
  • BDD / Bdd — pub struct Bdd(u32): an opaque index handle into the node table of one BddManager. Not an Rc; no per-node refcount cycles. Two Bdd are function-equal iff u32-equal (see canonicity). Never share a Bdd across managers.
  • BddManager — owner of node table, unique table, computed cache, and the variable order; &mut self for any operation that may allocate a node (ite, and, restrict, …), &self for pure reads (not, satcount, node_count). All Bdds are interpreted only relative to their manager.
  • node — one decision vertex: (level, low child, high child); the two terminals are the constants. node_count(f) = reachable nodes from f (a function-size measure / blow-up signal).
  • level — u32 rank of a variable in the fixed total order (0 = topmost/root-most). ithvar(level) returns the projection BDD for that level. Lower level number = nearer the root = tested earlier. Present/next pairing is encoded by adjacent levels (see VarMap, rename).
  • low child / high child — the two successors of a node: edge taken when the level's variable is 0 (low / else) vs 1 (high / then). ite(var, high, low) reconstructs the node.
  • reduction rules — (1) no duplicate node: never two structurally identical (level,low,high) nodes (enforced by the unique table); (2) no redundant node: delete any node with low == high (its variable is irrelevant). Together with the fixed order they yield canonicity.
  • unique table (hashcons) — hash map (level, low, high) -> Bdd consulted on every node creation; returns the existing handle if present, else allocates one. The mechanism that enforces the reduction rules at construction time. Hashconsing = "create node only if not already interned." Load-bearing: a miss that should have hit silently breaks canonicity.
  • computed cache — memo table keyed by operation operands (e.g. (f,g,h) for ite) -> result Bdd. Turns the otherwise exponential recursion of apply/ite into O(|f|*|g|*|h|). May be lossy (entries evictable) without affecting correctness, only speed. Distinct from the unique table (which must be exact).
  • canonicity — THE load-bearing invariant: fixed total order + both reduction rules => exactly one ROBDD per Boolean function, hence structural handle equality iff function equality and O(1) equality. Any bug breaking canonicity corrupts equality and fixpoint convergence -> wrong verdict with no crash (the gravest failure class).

BDD operations (all in BddManager)

  • ite / apply — ite(f,g,h) = (f AND g) OR (NOT f AND h): the single primitive of smv-bdd. "apply" = the general binary-operation algorithm; here every binary op is a special case of ite. and(f,g)=ite(f,g,⊥), or(f,g)=ite(f,⊤,g), xor, imp, not likewise. Memoized in the computed cache.
  • not / complement — not(f): logical negation, &self (pure), O(1) via a complement edge or a cache lookup (no full traversal).
  • cofactor / restrict — restrict(f, level, val): substitute a variable by a constant (f|_{x=val}), i.e. the Shannon cofactor. Building block of quantification and image.
  • cube — a BDD that is a conjunction of literals (one variable per level, each fixed to 0 or 1), representing a set of variables to quantify or a single assignment. Passed to exists/forall/and_exists to name the variable block; also the form a StateAssignment decodes from.
  • existential quantification — exists(f, cube) = ∃ vars(cube). f = OR of cofactors over those vars. Used to project away present (or next) variables in image/pre-image.
  • universal quantification — forall(f, cube) = ∀ vars(cube). f = AND of cofactors; dual of exists.
  • relational product (and_exists) — and_exists(f, g, cube) = ∃ vars(cube). (f AND g): conjoin then existentially quantify in one fused pass, never materializing the full f AND g. The hot kernel of image computation; pairs with early quantification to keep intermediates small.
  • rename — rename(f, map: &VarMap): relabel variables present<->next via a VarMap (level permutation). Used to convert an image expressed over next variables back to present variables (the [next->present] step).
  • VarMap — a level permutation/substitution table mapping present variable levels to their next-state counterparts (and back); fields present/next of EncodedFsm are VarMaps consumed by rename.
  • satcount — satcount(f, nvars) -> u128: number of satisfying assignments (models) of f over nvars variables; used as a reachable-state count / state-space-size metric (reported by bdd-stats).
  • node_count — node_count(f) -> usize: reachable node count of f; the per-function size metric for blow-up detection and benchmarks.
  • mark-sweep GC — reclamation of dead nodes (unreferenced Bdds) from the node table + unique table; mark live roots, sweep the rest, rehash. Triggered by table pressure; must preserve every externally held handle.
  • dot export — render a Bdd to Graphviz .dot for inspection/debugging (smv-cli dot); not on any verification hot path.
  • dynamic variable reordering (sifting) — runtime permutation of the variable order to shrink total node count (Rudell's sifting: slide each variable to its best level via adjacent swaps). Preserves the function (and thus all verdicts); only changes representation size. Optional, Phase 12.

Modeling language & encoding

  • Kripke structure — the finite-state semantic model (S, S₀, R, L): states, initial states, transition relation, AP-labeling. The SMV model denotes one; CTL/LTL are interpreted over it. Realized symbolically as EncodedFsm.
  • SmvModule — parsed SMV AST (smv-syntax): vars, defines, assigns, trans, invar, specs. Pre-encoding; finite-domain, not yet Boolean.
  • VarType — Boolean | Enum(Vec<Atom>) | Range(i64,i64): the finite domains a state variable may take.
  • VarEncoding — { levels: Vec<u32>, ty: VarType }: maps one finite-domain variable to its assigned Boolean BDD levels. Enum/scalar uses ceil(log2(|domain|)) bits plus an out-of-range constraint BDD; bounded Range similarly. Produced by smv-model's Encoder.
  • initial predicate — init: Bdd in EncodedFsm: the characteristic function of the set of initial states (init() assignments AND INVAR). A spec holds from the states satisfying this.
  • transition relation — trans: Bdd over present+next variables: characteristic function of R ⊆ S×S, built as next() AND TRANS AND next-INVAR. The relation image/pre-image quantify against. Stored monolithically or conjunctively partitioned.
  • EncodedFsm — the symbolic Kripke structure: { present: VarMap, next: VarMap, init: Bdd, trans: Bdd, invar: Bdd, fairness: Vec<Bdd>, ap: Vec<(Atom,Bdd)> }. The single object every checker (CtlChecker, LtlChecker, Unroller) consumes.
  • conjunctive partitioning — represent trans as a list of cluster BDDs whose conjunction is the full relation, instead of one monolithic BDD, so and_exists can apply early quantification per cluster. Keeps intermediate BDDs small; the standard scaling lever for the image step. (Disjunctive partitioning is the dual for asynchronous models.)
  • early quantification — interleave existential quantification with the conjunction order so each present variable is quantified as soon as no remaining trans cluster mentions it, minimizing peak node_count during image. Scheduled in Phase 12.

Reachability & fixpoints

  • image — Img(S) = ( ∃ present. ( trans(present,next) AND S(present) ) )[next->present]: the set of successors of state set S; computed via and_exists + rename. Forward step of reachability.
  • pre-image — dual of image: predecessors of S, quantifying next variables; preimage(S) = ∃ next. (trans AND S[present->next]). sat(EX p) = preimage(p).
  • reachable set — reachable() -> Bdd = lfp Z. init OR Img(Z): least set closed under image containing initial predicate; iterate image until the state-set Bdd stops changing (handle-equal to previous). Computed once, reused; reported by reachable/bdd-stats.
  • least fixpoint (lfp) — smallest set closed under a monotone BDD operator, reached by iterating from ⊥ upward to convergence (equality of consecutive Bdd handles). Used by reachable and CTL EU.
  • greatest fixpoint (gfp) — largest such set, iterated from ⊤ downward. Used by CTL EG and Emerson-Lei fair-state computation.
  • frontier simplification — restrict the next iterate to newly reached states (the frontier) rather than the whole set before the next image, shrinking the operand BDD; an optimization of the reachability loop.

Temporal logic & checking

  • CTL (Computation Tree Logic) — branching-time logic; modalities pair a path quantifier (A/E) with a temporal op (X/U/G/F). CtlFormula core = Ap/Not/And/Or/Ex/Eg/Eu; AX/AU/AG/AF/EF are derived. Each subformula evaluates to a state-set Bdd.
  • CtlChecker — smv-check engine: check(&CtlFormula) -> Bdd returns the SAT state-set; sat(EX p)=preimage(p), sat(E[p U q])=lfp Z. q OR (p AND EX Z), sat(EG p)=gfp Z. p AND EX Z. A spec holds iff init ⊆ sat(f), checked as (init AND not sat(f)) == bdd_false.
  • LTL (Linear Temporal Logic) — linear-time logic over single paths; LtlFormula = Ap/Not/And/Or/X/U/R/F/G. Checked via automata, not direct fixpoint, hence the Buchi path.
  • Buchi automaton — ω-automaton accepting infinite words that visit an accepting set infinitely often. smv-logic translates ¬φ (LTL) to one (GPVW/tableau or VWAA); M ⊨ Aφ iff M × Buchi(¬φ) has no reachable fair cycle.
  • generalized Buchi — Buchi with several acceptance sets, each visited infinitely often (the natural output of LTL translation, e.g. one set per Until). Either degeneralized to a single set or fed directly to Emerson-Lei.
  • LtlChecker — smv-check engine: builds the symbolic product of EncodedFsm and the Buchi automaton (automaton state bits + transition Bdd), then runs Emerson-Lei; counterexample = a fair lasso.
  • fair cycle — a reachable cycle (strongly connected, infinite path) that intersects every fairness/acceptance set infinitely often. Its existence = an accepting run = an LTL counterexample.
  • fairness — constraints forcing certain conditions to hold infinitely often along admitted paths; fairness: Vec<Bdd> in EncodedFsm. Buchi (visit set infinitely often) and Streett (response: □◇p → □◇q) variants supported (Phase 12).
  • Emerson-Lei — the fixpoint procedure computing the set of states on a fair cycle: a nested gfp/lfp over EX (pre-image) and the acceptance/fairness sets. Empty intersection with reachable initials => no fair cycle => spec holds.

Counterexamples & BMC

  • counterexample / witness — a concrete computation demonstrating a verdict: counterexample refutes a universal/safety claim (e.g. AG p), witness confirms an existential one (e.g. EF p, EG p). Extracted by walking onion-ring backward sets; rendered as a Trace.
  • lasso — a finite "stem" path to a cycle plus the cycle ("loop"), i.e. an ultimately-periodic infinite path; the shape of LTL / EG counterexamples. Encoded in Trace by loopback: Option<usize> (index where the loop closes).
  • Trace — { states: Vec<StateAssignment>, loopback: Option<usize> }: an executable counterexample/witness path; loopback = Some(i) marks a lasso looping to state i, None = finite prefix.
  • StateAssignment — one state of a Trace decoded from a BDD cube back to original SMV variables (VarType-typed values), not raw Boolean bits; the human-readable output unit.
  • bounded model checking (BMC) — smv-bmc: search for a counterexample of length ≤ k by unrolling the transition relation into a single propositional formula and asking a SAT solver. Finds shallow bugs fast; complete only at the completeness threshold.
  • unrolling — instantiating the transition relation over k time-indexed copies s0..sk: I(s0) AND conj_{i<k} T(si,si+1) AND (¬property over k); built by the Unroller. For G p, a length-k counterexample exists iff this is SAT.
  • Tseitin transform — linear-size conversion of an arbitrary Boolean formula to equisatisfiable CNF by introducing one fresh variable per subformula; how Unroller produces a CnfFormula without exponential blow-up.
  • CNF / DIMACS — Conjunctive Normal Form (AND of OR-of-literals); DIMACS = the standard integer-literal clause encoding. CnfFormula { clauses: Vec<Vec<i32>>, nvars: u32 } carries it; positive/negative i32 = literal polarity, 0 = clause terminator in serialized DIMACS.
  • SatBackend — trait fn solve(&mut self, cnf: &CnfFormula) -> SatResult: DIMACS in / assignment out boundary so #277 (or a bundled DPLL/CDCL) plugs in. smv-bmc does not implement a competitive solver. SatResult = Sat(Vec<bool>) | Unsat; a Sat model decodes to a Trace.
  • completeness threshold — the smallest k at which BMC becomes complete for a safety property: once k reaches the reachability/recurrence diameter, UNSAT at k proves the property for all depths. Computed/bounded in Phase 12; until reached, UNSAT only means "no counterexample of length ≤ k."

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Single-table view of all 14 phases (doc headings ## 12–## 25). Durations sum to the Section 1 timeline (26 weeks / ~6 months, single engineer, Mujir Qureshi). "Exit Criteria" are the measurable gates that promote a phase; failing any one blocks the next phase. Risks are ranked by blast radius; the recurring gravest class is silent canonicity corruption (wrong verdict, no crash).

Phase Doc Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria (measurable)
0 — Skeleton ## 12 Cargo workspace + 7 smv-* crate shells; Bdd/BddManager + AST + CtlFormula/LtlFormula shells; ParseError/ParseErrorKind; CI + Makefile + Dockerfile; smoke test 1 wk over-scoping shells into real impl; no_std+alloc not wired on smv-bdd/syntax/model/logic; circular crate deps cargo build --workspace + cargo test green in CI; smoke: ithvar(0) returns a node, parse a 1-var model, assert bdd_true() != bdd_false(); 4 inner crates compile no_std; dep graph acyclic (matches diagram)
1 — ROBDD core ## 13 node table + unique table (hashcons on (level,low,high)); 2 reduction rules; ite as the single primitive; not/and/or/xor/imp via ite; terminals 2 wk canonicity break (redundant/duplicate node escapes table → silent wrong equality); hash collisions corrupting nodes; complement-edge sign bugs canonicity property test passes 100k random formulas (handle-eq ⇔ truth-table-eq); no redundant node (low==high) and no duplicate (level,low,high) ever stored (asserted); ite ⇔ truth table on all 3-var inputs (256 cases)
2 — BDD ops ## 14 restrict (cofactor), exists/forall over a cube, and_exists (relational product), rename (present↔next), satcount, node_count, computed cache, mark-sweep GC, dot export 2 wk GC freeing live nodes (handle dangling); stale computed-cache entries after GC; and_exists ≠ and+exists (early-quant bug) and_exists(f,g,cube) ⇔ exists(and(f,g),cube) on 10k randoms; satcount ⇔ brute force for n≤16; GC reclaims ≥99% dead nodes, 0 live freed (canary test); cache cleared/rehomed across GC; dot reparses to identical BDD
3 — SMV language ## 15 Lexer+recursive-descent Parser → SmvModule AST (VAR boolean/enum/range; ASSIGN init/next/plain; DEFINE; INVAR; TRANS; CTLSPEC/LTLSPEC); name+type check; CircularDefine detection 2 wk grammar ambiguity (case/next precedence); missed CircularDefine → infinite expand; poor ParseErrorKind localization parses NuSMV corpus subset (≥20 models) to expected AST; every ParseErrorKind variant has a fixture (incl. CircularDefine, OutOfRange, TypeMismatch); type checker rejects ill-typed exprs with line/col; round-trip print⇒reparse stable
4 — Boolean encoding ## 16 VarEncoding for boolean, enum/scalar (ceil(log2) bits + out-of-range constraint), bounded Range; present/next VarMaps; compile Expr→Bdd over present (+ next for next()) 2 wk bit-width off-by-one (enum size not power of 2 → illegal codes admitted); present/next level interleaving wrong (hurts node count); arithmetic on Range overflow encoded var domain size == declared (out-of-range cubes ⇒ bdd_false in constraint); Expr→BDD ⇔ ground-truth eval on all assignments for n≤14 bits; interleaved present/next ordering verified; enum with non-2^k size has all illegal codes constrained out
5 — Symbolic FSM ## 17 EncodedFsm: init (init()+INVAR), trans (next() ∧ TRANS ∧ next-INVAR), fairness; conjunctive partitioning of trans 1.5 wk monolithic trans BDD blowup; INVAR not applied to both present & next; partition cluster order bad init/trans BDDs built for all corpus models; trans stored as conjunctive partition (≥2 clusters when
6 — Reachability ## 18 forward image Img(S)=(∃present.(trans∧S))[next→present], backward preimage, reachable lfp, frontier simplification; fixpoint framework reused by CTL 2 wk fixpoint non-convergence (canonicity-dependent); frontier vs full-set confusion; quantification of wrong var set reachable() = lfp Z. init ∨ Img(Z) converges (BDD stops changing) on all corpus models; satcount(reachable) matches NuSMV reachable-state count exactly; preimage is dual of image (quantify next) verified on 1k randoms
7 — CTL checking ## 19 CtlChecker::check → state-set Bdd: EX p=preimage; E[p U q]=lfp; EG p=gfp; derive EF/AG/AF/AX/AU; spec holds iff init ∧ ¬sat(f) == bdd_false 2 wk lfp vs gfp swapped (EU↔EG); derived-operator De Morgan error; "holds" test inverted EX/EU/EG match hand-computed sat-sets on ≥10 fixtures; all derived ops verified via duality; verdict ⇔ NuSMV on CTL corpus (same true/false); EG gfp and EU lfp both converge
8 — Counterexamples ## 20 onion-ring backward sets → shortest CTL counterexample/witness; lasso witness for EG; BDD cube → readable StateAssignment over original SMV vars 1.5 wk non-shortest trace; cube→var decode loses enum labels; lasso loopback index off Trace length == BDD distance (shortest, verified vs onion rings); every state decodes to a full StateAssignment over original vars (no raw bits); EG trace has valid loopback forming a real cycle in trans; trace replays satisfying property
9 — LTL→Büchi ## 21 LtlFormula parse + NNF; GPVW/tableau (or VWAA) → (generalized) Büchi automaton; symbolic encoding (state bits + transition Bdd) 2 wk incorrect Büchi (wrong language); acceptance-set encoding wrong; state-bit explosion automaton language ⇔ formula on bounded-word check (all words ≤ len 8) for ≥10 LTL formulas; NNF idempotent; generalized acceptance sets correct count; symbolic transition BDD ⇔ explicit automaton edges
10 — LTL checking ## 22 product of EncodedFsm × Büchi automaton; fair-cycle detection via Emerson-Lei gfp; counterexample = fair lasso 2 wk fair-cycle missed/false (Emerson-Lei nesting bug); product var clash; ¬φ automaton used incorrectly "A φ holds ⇔ product(model, Büchi(¬φ)) has no reachable fair cycle" verified; Emerson-Lei fair-states gfp converges; verdict ⇔ NuSMV on LTL corpus; fair lasso replays as a real accepting run
11 — BMC ## 23 Unroller: I(s0) ∧ ⋀ T(si,si+1) ∧ ¬prop_k → CnfFormula via Tseitin; SatBackend trait + bundled DPLL/CDCL (or #277 hook); incremental k; SAT model→Trace 2.5 wk Tseitin polarity/equisat bug; off-by-one in k unroll; SatBackend DIMACS mismatch (1-based lits) unrolled CNF equisatisfiable with semantics (checked vs BDD reachability for k≤diameter); SatResult::Sat model decodes to a valid length-k Trace; BMC finds same shortest CEX length as BDD onion ring; DIMACS round-trips through SatBackend
12 — Optimizations + fairness ## 24 dynamic var reordering (sifting), conjunctive/disjunctive partitioned trans, early-quantification schedule, cache tuning, Büchi/Streett fairness; BMC completeness threshold 2.5 wk sifting breaks canonicity (handles invalidated mid-reorder); early-quant schedule changes result; wrong completeness threshold → unsound "no CEX" sifting reduces peak node_count ≥30% on ≥3 fixtures with identical verdicts pre/post; verdicts unchanged after every optimization (regression vs Phase 7/10); BMC declares completeness only at recurrence/reachability diameter (sound, checked vs full reachability)
13 — Validation ## 25 differential testing vs NuSMV on model+spec corpus (same verdict + comparable CEX); BDD property/fuzz tests; reach/CTL/LTL conformance; benchmarks vs targets — the "done" gate 1 wk undetected divergence from NuSMV; benchmarks miss perf targets; fuzzers too shallow to hit canonicity bugs 100% verdict agreement with NuSMV across full corpus (≥50 model+spec pairs); BDD fuzzer (apply⇔truth-table, canonicity) runs ≥1M ops, 0 failures; reachable-state counts match NuSMV exactly; perf within Section 1 targets (state-space size, node counts, time, memory)

Duration roll-up: 1+2+2+2+2+1.5+2+2+1.5+2+2+2.5+2.5+1 = 26 weeks, matching the Section 1 timeline.

Definition of "done" (two gates)

Phase-0 done (## 12 exit) means the scaffold is real and the engine is not: the 7-crate smv-* workspace compiles and CI is green, the four inner crates are no_std+alloc, Bdd/BddManager/the SMV AST/CtlFormula/LtlFormula exist as compilable shells, error types are defined, and the smoke test passes (ithvar(0) builds a node, a 1-var model parses, bdd_true() != bdd_false()). No ROBDD reduction, no fixpoint, no CTL/LTL/BMC checking is implemented — only the skeleton stands.

Full done (## 25 exit) means the symbolic model checker is correct and competitive against the canonical reference: ROBDD canonicity holds under ≥1M fuzz operations; symbolic reachability, CTL (EX/EU/EG + derived), LTL (Büchi product + Emerson-Lei fair cycle), and SAT-based BMC all produce verdicts and counterexample Traces that agree with NuSMV on the entire model+spec corpus (≥50 pairs, 100% verdict match, comparable counterexamples), reachable-state counts match exactly, and all performance targets from Section 1 (peak BDD node_count, reachable-state size, time, memory) are met — with the load-bearing invariant, ROBDD canonicity, never silently violated. This gate is what earns the — Rust ✅ tag in the future coding phase; it is not claimed during blueprint authoring.
